The Republican Party is a [right-wing populist](8354382.md) and [neo-nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-nationalism) party.[^cite_note-populism-228] Until 2017, it was generally described as upholding [American conservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatism_in_the_United_States), with conservatives now only making up a minority of the party.[^cite_note-IdeologyConservatism-14]

[Comparative politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comparative_politics) studies by the [V-Dem Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V-Dem_Institute),[^cite_note-Lührmann-2020-24] [Polity Data Series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polity_data_series),[^cite_note-229][^cite_note-Polity_Data_Series-2025-230] [Global Party Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pippa_Norris),[^cite_note-Guillemsource-231] [Protect Democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protect_Democracy),[^cite_note-232] *[The Washington Post](102226.md)*,[^cite_note-Tharoor-2021-29] and the [Manifesto Project Database](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manifesto_Project_Database)[^cite_note-233] classify the party as "[far-right](221220.md)" in an international context, as well as a majority of political scientists.[^cite_note-241] [Freedom House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_House) described "the Republicans on the [right](56522.md) and the Democrats on the [left](18499.md)" in its 2026 report for the United States.[^cite_note-242] The political theorist [Jan-Werner Müller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jan-Werner_M%C3%BCller) argued in 2026 that the Republican Party had "alarmingly radicalized" to a point of being further right than the far-right [Alternative for Germany](38708229.md) (AfD), but because of the U.S.' established [two-party system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two-party_system), "still gives many people the impression of being "mainstream"."[^cite_note-Jan-240]

By the early 2020s, the Republican Party distanced itself from its traditional international conservative allies and aligned itself with the global far-right,[^cite_note-Rovira_Kaltwasser-78][^cite_note-Tharoor-2021-29][^cite_note-Sunshine-2026-30] supporting AfD, [Fidesz](404647.md), and the [National Rally](67119.md).[^cite_note-Colom-2024-28]

### Embrace of Trumpism and far-right ideology

See also: [Trumpism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trumpism) and [Radical right (United States)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radical_right_\(United_States\))

The election of Trump in 2016 saw the Republican Party shift to embrace and bring [far-right](221220.md), fringe, and extreme ideas and organizations into the mainstream.[^cite_note-243] His election shifted traditional Republican beliefs and ideology into a new leadership style and political agenda referred to as Trumpism.[^cite_note-Ball_2024-7][^cite_note-v075-8][^cite_note-i277-9]

Trump explicitly and routinely disparages racial, religious, and ethnic minorities,[^cite_note-244] and scholars consistently find that racial animus regarding blacks, immigrants, and Muslims are the best predictors of support for Trump.[^cite_note-Berman-245] By 2025, congressional Republicans have increasingly used overtly bigoted language and offensive tropes against the racial and religious identity of their political opponents with little to no pushback from GOP leadership.[^cite_note-246][^cite_note-247] In 2026, elected Republicans have increasingly used blatantly [anti-Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Muslim) rhetoric which was described by commentators as mirroring a prior increase in such rhetoric by the GOP following the [9/11 attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/9/11_attacks) in 2001.[^cite_note-248][^cite_note-249][^cite_note-250]

The [second presidency of Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_presidency_of_Trump) saw him nominate several White House officials with ties to [antisemitic](1078.md) extremists.[^cite_note-251] His administration promoted social media content promoting [remigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Remigration)[^cite_note-Yousef-2025-37] and containing antisemitic slurs,[^cite_note-252] and was criticized as promoting [Nazism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazism)[^cite_note-253][^cite_note-254][^cite_note-255][^cite_note-256] and [white nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_nationalism),[^cite_note-SPLC_2025-257][^cite_note-258] the latter of which has become influential within the party since the 2020s.[^cite_note-WhiteNat-34] Other social media posts from multiple government agencies have variously alluded to [neo-Nazi](54361.md) and [white supremacist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_supremacist) literature and promoted [ethnic cleansing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_cleansing) and [QAnon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/QAnon) conspiracies.[^cite_note-259] The [Southern Poverty Law Center](27164.md) found that some "images and language appear to come directly from antisemitic and neo-Nazi publications and a white Christian nationalist website".[^cite_note-SPLC_2025-257]

According to *The New York Times*, Republican youth groups have grown increasingly divided over the acceptance and prevalence of far-right speakers and ideology.[^cite_note-260] In October 2025, *Politico* [revealed leaked group chats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Young_Republican_group_chat_leaks) among high-ranking leaders of the "[Young Republicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Young_Republicans)" that included language praising [Adolf Hitler](2731583.md) and promoting Nazism, encouraging the rape and killing of political opponents, extensive use of antisemitic and racial slurs, and favorable opinions on [slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery).[^cite_note-261] The messages drew bipartisan condemnation, and [another incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Taylor_swastika_incident) involving the display of an American flag with a [swastika](45943.md) in Republican Congressman [David Taylor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Taylor_\(Ohio_politician\))'s office a day later spurred significant political commentary about the future of the GOP and condemnation about the prominence of Nazi ideology within the party.[^cite_note-262] The same month, *Politico* posted more leaked messages from Republican Office of Special Council nominee [Paul Ingrassia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Ingrassia_\(lawyer\)) where he stated he had "a Nazi streak", that he wanted white men in positions of leadership, used ethnic slurs for Black people and Italians, and proposed making [Kwanzaa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kwanzaa) and all celebrations of Black culture illegal.[^cite_note-263] In March 2026, group chats among young Republicans created by the [Miami-Dade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miami-Dade_County,_Florida) Republican Party included slurs against Black and Jewish people, killing African-Americans, and Hitler's policies.[^cite_note-264] *Politico* reported that several more scandals in 2026 involving Republican officials making controversial remarks led to concerns over the influence of "[Groypers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Groypers)" and far-right ideology among young Republicans, with the conservative [Manhattan Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manhattan_Institute) finding 31% of Republicans under the age of 50 self-identified as having "racist" views.[^cite_note-265]

Starting in October 2025, *[Axios](52436164.md)*, *Politico*, *[The Economist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist)*, *[The Wall Street Journal](173070.md)*, and *[The New York Times](30680.md)* described the party as undergoing a "rift" among a growing cohort of anti-Jewish and antisemitic voices within the party and debate over how expansive the party's coalition should be.[^cite_note-Economist_12232025-266][^cite_note-267] In January 2026, Trump publicly came out against those with antisemitic views in the party, which *The New York Times* and *The Economist* described as being contrasted with J.D. Vance's prior refusal to condemn the incidents and statement that the party did not need "purity tests".[^cite_note-Economist_12232025-266][^cite_note-268]

Beginning in the early 2020s, several Republican politicians have been promoting the [Great Replacement conspiracy theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Replacement_conspiracy_theory).[^cite_note-269]

### Economic policies

[Enacting high tariffs](78448911.md) on foreign imports is a core component of [Donald Trump](4848272.md)'s fiscal agenda. Tariffs are taxes on foreign imports, mainly paid by domestic businesses, given that consumers generally do not import foreign goods directly.[^cite_note-Buckle_Up-270] By raising tariffs to their highest levels since the [Gilded Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gilded_Age), Trump enacted one of the largest tax increases by any Republican president.[^cite_note-Trump_Is_Raising_Them-271] The Constitution's [Import-Export Clause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Import-Export_Clause) requires that only the federal government be allowed to collect tariff revenue from imports.[^cite_note-Gilded_Age_vision-272]

Historically, Republicans advocated for [free markets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_market) and individual achievement as the primary factors behind economic prosperity.[^cite_note-273] Reduction in income taxes for those with higher incomes[^cite_note-274][^cite_note-275] is a core component of Republicans' fiscal agenda.[^cite_note-276]

In February 2026, the *[New York Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Times)* contrasted Trump's economic policies with those of [Mitt Romney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitt_Romney), the previous Republican presidential nominee before Trump. It described Trump's economic ideology as similar to *[dirigisme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dirigisme)*, used in some countries such as [France](5843419.md).[^cite_note-277]

#### State capitalism

The relationship between income and voting broke down in the 2010s. Instead, age and education became the best predictors of voting behavior.[^cite_note-278] Writing for *[Time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_\(magazine\))* magazine in 2024, [Jeffrey Sonnenfeld](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeffrey_Sonnenfeld) described Trump's moves against capitalism as sharing "more in common with far-left progressive positions than with traditional GOP views, and are often far more progressive than the Biden Administration" and wrote that he expected such attacks to continue into his second term.[^cite_note-279]

By 2025, Trump and the Republican Party largely abandoned traditional Republican orthodoxy about protecting and promoting the free market,[^cite_note-280] instead favoring [state capitalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_capitalism) by taking direct government equity stakes in major US corporations, an approach that has been characterized by critics in favor of free market economics as [socialist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist).[^cite_note-281] The Trump administration has also deviated from the GOP's conventional free market approach by ordering [Nvidia](39120.md) and [AMD](2400.md) to pay the federal government a portion of chip sales to China.[^cite_note-282]

#### Taxes and trade

As of 2026, the Republican Party supports near-universal [tariffs](55551.md), but that has not always been the case. For example, during the last half of the 20th century, Republicans were strong proponents of [free trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_trade). However 68% of Republicans and Republican-leaning independents approve of the increasing tariffs while the Democrats overwhelmingly disapprove of it at 89%.[^cite_note-283] The current Republican president, Donald Trump, has been a staunch proponent of enacting tariffs as a means of generating tax revenue and has been described as a [mercantilist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercantilism).[^cite_note-Helleiner_2021-153][^cite_note-284] In 2025, Trump raised American tariff rates to the highest in the world, at the highest level since the [Smoot–Hawley Tariff Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smoot%E2%80%93Hawley_Tariff_Act) of 1930.[^cite_note-global_trade_war-285] Donald Trump opposes [globalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_globalization), and his economic policies have been described as attempting to unravel the multilateral global economic order, including the power of the [World Trade Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Organization) (WTO).[^cite_note-Trump's_aggressive_push-154] Trump has expressed his admiration for Republican president [William McKinley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_McKinley)'s tariff policies. McKinley was the author of the [Tariff Act of 1890](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tariff_Act_of_1890), and both Trump and McKinley nicknamed themselves as a "Tariff Man".[^cite_note-President_McKinley-286][^cite_note-Buckle_Up-270] According to an April 2025 Economist/[YouGov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/YouGov) poll, "Republican voters overwhelmingly support Trump's tariffs, while Democratic voters generally do not."[^cite_note-favor_Trump_tariffs-287]

At its inception, the Republican Party supported [protective tariffs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tariffs_in_United_States_history). Abraham Lincoln enacted tariffs during the Civil War.[^cite_note-Coy_F._Cross_II_2012_45-288][^cite_note-Platform-289] The great battle over the high [Payne–Aldrich Tariff Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Payne%E2%80%93Aldrich_Tariff_Act) in 1910 caused a split in the party.[^cite_note-290] The [Reciprocal Tariff Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reciprocal_Tariff_Act) of 1934 marked a sharp departure from the era of [protectionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protectionism) in the United States. American duties on foreign products declined from an average of 46% in 1934 to 12% by 1962, which included the presidency of Republican president [Dwight D. Eisenhower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dwight_D._Eisenhower).[^cite_note-Bailey-291] After World War II, the U.S. promoted the [General Agreement on Tariffs and Trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Agreement_on_Tariffs_and_Trade) (GATT) established in 1947, to minimize tariffs and other restrictions, and to liberalize trade among all capitalist countries.[^cite_note-barton-292][^cite_note-293] During the [Reagan](25433.md) and [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush) administrations, Republicans abandoned protectionist policies[^cite_note-Karagiannis-294] and came out against quotas and in favor of the GATT and the [World Trade Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Organization) policy of minimal economic barriers to global trade. Free trade with Canada came about as a result of the [Canada–United States Free Trade Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada%E2%80%93United_States_Free_Trade_Agreement) of 1987, which led in 1994 to the [North American Free Trade Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_American_Free_Trade_Agreement) (NAFTA) based on Reagan's plan to enlarge the scope of the market for American firms to include Canada and Mexico. President [Bill Clinton](3356.md), with strong Republican support in 1993, pushed NAFTA through Congress over the vehement objection of labor unions.[^cite_note-295][^cite_note-296]

The 2016 presidential election marked a return to supporting protectionism, beginning with [Donald Trump's first presidency](52231341.md).[^cite_note-297][^cite_note-Swanson-298] In 2017, only 36% of Republicans agreed that free trade agreements are good for the United States, compared to 67% of Democrats. When asked if free trade has helped respondents specifically, the approval numbers for Democrats drop to 54%, however approval ratings among Republicans remain relatively unchanged at 34%.[^cite_note-299]

Income tax cuts have been at the core of Republican economic policy since 1980.[^cite_note-300] At the national level and state level, Republicans tend to pursue policies of tax cuts and deregulation.[^cite_note-Grumbach-2021-301] Modern Republicans advocate the theory of [supply-side economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supply_side_economics), which holds that lower tax rates increase economic growth.[^cite_note-302] Many Republicans oppose [higher tax rates for higher earners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressive_taxation), which they believe are unfairly targeted at those who create jobs and wealth. They believe private spending is more efficient than government spending. Republican lawmakers have also sought to limit funding for tax enforcement and [tax collection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revenue_service).[^cite_note-How_the_IRS_Was_Gutted-303]

The modern Republican Party's economic policy positions tend to align with business interests and the affluent.[^cite_note-304][^cite_note-305][^cite_note-306][^cite_note-307][^cite_note-308]

Republicans have traditionally advocated in favor of [fiscal conservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiscal_conservatism).[^cite_note-309][^cite_note-310][^cite_note-311] By the 2020s, Republicans have largely abandoned fiscal conservatism as an ideological cornerstone.[^cite_note-312]

#### Labor unions and the minimum wage

The Republican Party is generally opposed to labor unions.[^cite_note-313][^cite_note-314][^cite_note-New_Fusionism-104] Republicans believe corporations should be able to establish their own employment practices, including benefits and wages, with the free market deciding the price of work. Since the 1920s, Republicans have generally been opposed by [labor union](17626.md) organizations and members. At the national level, Republicans supported the [Taft–Hartley Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taft%E2%80%93Hartley_Act) of 1947, which gives workers the right not to participate in unions. Modern Republicans at the state level generally support various [right-to-work laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right-to-work_laws).[^cite_note-316] Most Republicans also oppose increases in the [minimum wage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minimum_wage).[^cite_note-317]

#### Environmental policies

Main article: [Political positions of the Republican Party § Environmental policies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_positions_of_the_Republican_Party#Environmental_policies)



Democrats and Republicans have diverged on the seriousness of the threat posed by climate change, with Republicans' assessment remaining essentially unchanged over the past decade.

[^cite_note-PewClimateChange_20230418-318]



Opinion about human causation of climate change increased substantially with education among Democrats, but not among Republicans.

[^cite_note-Pew_20220301-319]

&#x20;Conversely, opinions favoring becoming carbon neutral declined substantially with age among Republicans, but not among Democrats.

[^cite_note-Pew_20220301-319]

Historically, [progressive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressivism_in_the_United_States) leaders in the Republican Party supported [environmental protection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_protection). Republican President [Theodore Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodore_Roosevelt) was a prominent [conservationist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservation_\(ethic\)) whose policies eventually led to the creation of the [National Park Service](161535.md).[^cite_note-Filler-320] While Republican President [Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nixon) was not an environmentalist, he signed legislation to create the [Environmental Protection Agency](58666.md) in 1970 and had a comprehensive environmental program.[^cite_note-321] However, this position has changed since the 1980s and the administration of President [Ronald Reagan](25433.md), who labeled environmental regulations a burden on the economy.[^cite_note-Dunlap_2010-322] Since then, Republicans have increasingly taken positions against environmental regulation,[^cite_note-323][^cite_note-324][^cite_note-325] with many Republicans rejecting the [scientific consensus on climate change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_consensus_on_climate_change).[^cite_note-Dunlap_2010-322][^cite_note-326][^cite_note-Ringquist-327][^cite_note-Shipan_Environmental_Policy-328] Republican voters are divided over the human causes of climate change and global warming.[^cite_note-329] Since 2008,[^cite_note-Davenport2017-330] many members of the Republican Party have been criticized for being [anti-environmentalist](8149492.md)[^cite_note-331][^cite_note-332][^cite_note-333] and promoting [climate change denial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_denial)[^cite_note-Dunlap_2010-322][^cite_note-334][^cite_note-chait-335] in opposition to the general scientific consensus, making them unique even among other worldwide conservative parties.[^cite_note-chait-335]

In 2006, then-[California Governor](62517.md) [Arnold Schwarzenegger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arnold_Schwarzenegger) broke from Republican orthodoxy to sign several bills imposing caps on [carbon emissions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_emissions) in California. Then-President [George W. Bush](3414021.md) opposed mandatory caps at a national level. Bush's decision not to regulate carbon dioxide as a pollutant was [challenged in the Supreme Court by 12 states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massachusetts_v._Environmental_Protection_Agency),[^cite_note-Landmark_Law-336] with the court ruling against the Bush administration in 2007.[^cite_note-337] Bush also publicly opposed ratification of the [Kyoto Protocols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyoto_Protocol)[^cite_note-Dunlap_2010-322][^cite_note-BushGW-338] which sought to limit greenhouse gas emissions and thereby [combat climate change](2119179.md); his position was heavily criticized by climate scientists.[^cite_note-339]

The Republican Party rejects [cap-and-trade](37104.md) policy to limit carbon emissions.[^cite_note-340] In the 2000s, Senator [John McCain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_McCain) proposed bills (such as the [McCain-Lieberman Climate Stewardship Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McCain-Lieberman_Climate_Stewardship_Act)) that would have regulated carbon emissions, but his position on climate change was unusual among high-ranking party members.[^cite_note-Dunlap_2010-322] Some Republican candidates have supported the development of [alternative fuels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alternative_fuel) to achieve [energy independence for the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._energy_independence). Some Republicans support increased [oil drilling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_well) in protected areas such as the [Arctic National Wildlife Refuge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arctic_National_Wildlife_Refuge), a position that has drawn criticism from activists.[^cite_note-341]

Many Republicans during the [presidency of Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Barack_Obama) opposed his administration's new environmental regulations, such as those on carbon emissions from coal. In particular, many Republicans supported building the [Keystone Pipeline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keystone_Pipeline); this position was supported by businesses, but opposed by indigenous peoples' groups and environmental activists.[^cite_note-342][^cite_note-343][^cite_note-344]

According to the [Center for American Progress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_for_American_Progress), a non-profit liberal advocacy group, more than 55% of congressional Republicans were [climate change deniers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_denial) in 2014.[^cite_note-msnbc20140512-345][^cite_note-charlestongazette20141222-346] [PolitiFact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PolitiFact) in May 2014 found "relatively few Republican members of Congress ... accept the prevailing scientific conclusion that [global warming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_warming) is both real and man-made." The group found eight members who acknowledged it, although the group acknowledged there could be more and that not all members of Congress have taken a stance on the issue.[^cite_note-347][^cite_note-348]

From 2008 to 2017, the Republican Party went from "debating how to combat human-caused climate change to arguing that it does not exist", according to *[The New York Times](30680.md)*.[^cite_note-Davenport2017-330] In January 2015, the Republican-led U.S. Senate voted 98–1 to pass a resolution acknowledging that "climate change is real and is not a hoax"; however, an amendment stating that "human activity significantly contributes to climate change" was supported by only five Republican senators.[^cite_note-349] By 2025, the GOP during the second Trump administration moved to abandon all efforts to regulate greenhouse gases by reversing the [endangerment finding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endangerment_finding), and Trump criticized global warming as a "hoax" and a "con job".[^cite_note-350] The Energy Department also released [a report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_Critical_Review_of_Impacts_of_Greenhouse_Gas_Emissions_on_the_U.S._Climate) promoting climate change denial and refuting the scientific consensus on climate change after several climate skeptics were given government jobs.[^cite_note-351]

#### Health care

The party opposes a [single-payer health care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Single-payer_health_care) system,[^cite_note-352][^cite_note-353] describing it as [socialized medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialized_medicine). It also opposes the [Affordable Care Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affordable_Care_Act)[^cite_note-354] and expansions of Medicaid.[^cite_note-355] Historically, there have been diverse and overlapping views within both the Republican Party and the Democratic Party on the role of government in health care, but the two parties became highly polarized on the topic during 2008–2009 and onwards.[^cite_note-Hacker-2010-356]

Both Republicans and [Democrats](5043544.md) made various proposals to establish federally funded aged health insurance prior to the bipartisan effort to establish [Medicare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicare_\(United_States\)) and [Medicaid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicaid) in 1965.[^cite_note-357][^cite_note-358][^cite_note-Zeitz-2017-359] No Republican member of Congress voted for the [Affordable Care Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affordable_Care_Act) in 2009, and after it passed, the party made frequent attempts to repeal it.[^cite_note-Hacker-2010-356][^cite_note-360] At the state level, the party has tended to adopt a position against [Medicaid expansion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicaid_expansion).[^cite_note-Grumbach-2021-301][^cite_note-Zeitz-2017-359]

Republicans typically believe individuals should take responsibility for their own circumstances and that the private sector is more effective in helping the poor through charity than the government is through welfare programs, and argue that social assistance programs cause government dependency.[^cite_note-361] As of November 2022, all 11 states that had not expanded Medicaid had Republican-controlled [state legislatures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_legislature_\(United_States\)).[^cite_note-KaiserMedicaid-362]

By the 2020s, Republican officials had increasingly adopted [anti-vaccine activism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-vaccine_activism) and policy.[^cite_note-363]

### Foreign policy

See also: [History of foreign policy and national defense in the Republican Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_foreign_policy_and_national_defense_in_the_Republican_Party)

The Republican Party has a persistent history of skepticism or opposition to [multilateralism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multilateralism) in [American foreign policy](7564.md).[^cite_note-364] Its members have frequently advocated for restricting [foreign aid](22557286.md) to assert the interests of the United States.[^cite_note-365][^cite_note-366][^cite_note-367] The modern GOP opposes [liberal internationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_internationalism).[^cite_note-368][^cite_note-369] Trump shifted the party towards an isolationist and nationalist agenda that is skeptical of international alliances and [free trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_trade).[^cite_note-370]

#### Israel



President Trump with the current Prime Minister of Israel, [Benjamin Netanyahu](56472.md) (July 2025)

During the 1940s, many Republicans, particularly Senator [Robert A. Taft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_A._Taft), advocated for recognition of Israel,[^cite_note-371] leading to support for Israel being integrated into the 1948 Republican Party platform.[^cite_note-372] Nevertheless, some Republicans at the time opposed the cause of an independent Jewish state due to the influence of [conservatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatism_in_the_United_States) of the [Old Right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Right_\(United_States\)).[^cite_note-Cavari-2020-373] The rise of [neoconservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoconservatism) saw the Republican Party become further pro-Israel by the 1990s and 2000s,[^cite_note-374] although notable anti-Israel sentiment persisted through [paleoconservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleoconservatism) figures such as [Pat Buchanan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pat_Buchanan).[^cite_note-375] As president, Donald Trump generally supported Israel during most of his term, but became increasingly critical of Israeli Prime Minister [Benjamin Netanyahu](56472.md) towards the end of it.[^cite_note-376] According to *[i24NEWS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I24NEWS_\(Israeli_TV_channel\))*, the 2020s have seen declining support for Israel among nationalist Republicans, led by individuals such as [Tucker Carlson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tucker_Carlson).[^cite_note-Cavari-2020-373][^cite_note-377] Nevertheless, the 2024 Republican Party platform reaffirmed the party would "stand with Israel" and called for the deportation of "pro-[Hamas](13913.md) radicals", while expressing a desire for peace in the Middle East.[^cite_note-378] Although the Republican Party has often positioned itself as an opponent of antisemitism and denounced Democrats as insufficiently supportive of Israel,[^cite_note-379] many members of the [Christian right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_right) support Israel primarily due to [theological beliefs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Zionism) about the centrality of Israel to the [Second Coming of Jesus Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Coming) and the conversion or [damnation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hell_in_Christianity) of Jews and other non-Christians.[^cite_note-380][^cite_note-381]

#### Taiwan

In its 2016 platform,[^cite_note-amazonaws1-382] the Republican Party's stance on [Taiwan](25734.md) was: "We oppose any unilateral steps by either side to alter the status quo in the Taiwan Straits on the principle that all issues regarding the island's future must be resolved peacefully, through dialogue, and be agreeable to the people of Taiwan." In addition, if "China were to violate those principles, the United States, in accord with the [Taiwan Relations Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan_Relations_Act), will help Taiwan defend itself".

Mention of Taiwan was omitted from the party's 2024 platform.[^cite_note-383]

#### War on terror

Main article: [War on terror](13425800.md)

Further information: [September 11 attacks](5058690.md)

Since the terrorist attacks on September 11, 2001, neoconservatives in the party have supported the war on terror, including the [War in Afghanistan](19666611.md) and the [Iraq War](5043324.md). The [George W. Bush administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_George_W._Bush) took the position that the [Geneva Conventions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva_Conventions) do not apply to [unlawful combatants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unlawful_combatant), while other prominent Republicans, such as [Ted Cruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ted_Cruz), strongly oppose the use of [enhanced interrogation techniques](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enhanced_interrogation_techniques), which they view as torture.[^cite_note-384] In the 2020s, Trumpist Republicans such as [Matt Gaetz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matt_Gaetz) supported reducing American military presence abroad and ending [intervention in countries such as Somalia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_military_intervention_in_Somalia_\(2007%E2%80%93present\)).[^cite_note-385]

#### Europe, Russia and Ukraine

See also: [United States and the Russian invasion of Ukraine](70376708.md)

The 2016 Republican platform eliminated references to giving weapons to [Ukraine](31750.md) in its fight with Russia and [rebel forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Separatist_forces_of_the_war_in_Donbass); the removal of this language reportedly resulted from intervention from staffers to presumptive Republican presidential nominee Donald Trump.[^cite_note-386] However, the Trump administration approved a new sale of anti-tank weapons to Ukraine in 2017.[^cite_note-387] Republicans generally question European [NATO](21133.md) members' alleged insufficient investment in defense funding, and some are dissatisfied with American aid to Ukraine.[^cite_note-388][^cite_note-389] Some Republican members of the U.S. Congress support foreign aid to Israel but not to Ukraine,[^cite_note-Falk_2023_t804-164][^cite_note-Riccardi-165] and have been described by U.S. media as [pro-Russian](2541825.md).[^cite_note-Lillis,_Ball,_&_Jonathan-169][^cite_note-Lange-171][^cite_note-New_York_Times-172][^cite_note-Baker-173][^cite_note-Cohn2023-174]

Amid the [Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine), several prominent Republicans criticized some colleagues and conservative media outlets for echoing Russian propaganda. [Liz Cheney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liz_Cheney), formerly the third-ranking House Republican, said "a [Putin](32817.md) wing of the Republican Party" had emerged. Former vice president [Mike Pence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Pence) said, "There is no room in the Republican Party for apologists for Putin." [House Foreign Affairs Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_Foreign_Affairs_Committee) chairman [Michael McCaul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_McCaul) asserted that Russian propaganda had "infected a good chunk of my party's base". [House Intelligence Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_Intelligence_Committee) chairman [Mike Turner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Turner) confirmed McCaul's assessment, asserting that some propaganda coming directly from Russia could be heard on the House floor. Republican senator [Thom Tillis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thom_Tillis) characterized the influential conservative commentator [Tucker Carlson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tucker_Carlson), who frequently expresses pro-Russia sentiments, as Russia's "[useful idiot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Useful_idiot)".[^cite_note-390][^cite_note-391][^cite_note-392][^cite_note-393]

In April 2024, a majority of Republican members of the [U.S. House of Representatives](19468510.md) voted against a military aid package to Ukraine.[^cite_note-394] Both Trump and Senator [JD Vance](53396477.md), the 2024 Republican presidential nominee and vice presidential nominee respectively, have been vocal critics of military aid to Ukraine and advocates of a peace deal between Russia and Ukraine.[^cite_note-395][^cite_note-396][^cite_note-397][^cite_note-398] The 2024 Republican Party platform did not mention Russia or Ukraine, but stated the party's objectives to "prevent World War III" and "restore peace to Europe".[^cite_note-399]

In February 2025, during the [Trump–Zelenskyy meeting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Trump%E2%80%93Zelenskyy_Oval_Office_meeting), Trump and Vance hostilely berated Ukrainian president [Volodymyr Zelenskyy](59210523.md).[^cite_note-400]

### Social issues

The Republican Party is generally associated with [social conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_conservative) policies, although it does have dissenting centrist and [libertarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libertarianism_in_the_United_States) factions. The social conservatives support laws that uphold their [traditional values](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_values), such as [opposition to same-sex marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opposition_to_same-sex_marriage_in_the_United_States), abortion, and marijuana.[^cite_note-Zelizer_2004_704–5-401] The Republican Party's positions on social and cultural issues are in part a reflection of the influential role that the [Christian right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_right) has had in the party since the 1970s.[^cite_note-402][^cite_note-403][^cite_note-Lewis-2019-192] Most conservative Republicans also oppose [gun control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gun_control), [affirmative action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affirmative_action), and [illegal immigration](7124412.md).[^cite_note-Zelizer_2004_704–5-401][^cite_note-404] By the 2020s, the GOP has shifted from traditional conservative support of limited government to instead support [statism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statism) and increased government oversight when enforcing [conservative cultural values](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_conservatism_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-Roads_to_Congress_2024-26]

#### Abortion and embryonic stem cell research

The Republican position on [abortion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_in_the_United_States) has changed significantly over time.[^cite_note-Williams-2022-194][^cite_note-Williams-2015-405] During the 1960s and early 1970s, opposition to abortion was concentrated among members of the political left and the Democratic Party; most liberal Catholics—which tended to vote for the Democratic Party—opposed expanding abortion access while most conservative evangelical Protestants supported it.[^cite_note-Williams-2015-405]

During this period, Republicans generally favored legalized abortion more than Democrats,[^cite_note-Halpern-2018-406][^cite_note-407] although significant heterogeneity could be found within both parties.[^cite_note-Taylor-2018-408] Leading Republican political figures, including [Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nixon), [Gerald Ford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerald_Ford), [Ronald Reagan](25433.md), and [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush), took pro-choice positions until the early 1980s.[^cite_note-Halpern-2018-406] However, starting at this point, both George H.W. Bush and Ronald Reagan described themselves as pro-life during their presidencies.

In the 21st century, both [George W. Bush](3414021.md)[^cite_note-409] and [Donald Trump](4848272.md) described themselves as "[pro-life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-abortion_movements)" during their terms. However, Trump stated that he supported the legality and ethics of abortion before his candidacy in 2015.[^cite_note-410]

Summarizing the rapid shift in the Republican and Democratic positions on abortion, Sue Halpern writes:[^cite_note-Williams-2022-194]

> ...in the late 1960s and early 1970s, many Republicans were behind efforts to liberalize and even decriminalize abortion; theirs was the party of reproductive choice, while Democrats, with their large Catholic constituency, were the opposition. Republican governor Ronald Reagan signed the California Therapeutic Abortion Act, one of the most liberal abortion laws in the country, in 1967, legalizing abortion for women whose mental or physical health would be impaired by pregnancy, or whose pregnancies were the result of rape or incest. The same year, the Republican strongholds of North Carolina and Colorado made it easier for women to obtain abortions. New York, under Governor [Nelson Rockefeller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nelson_Rockefeller), a Republican, eliminated all restrictions on women seeking to terminate pregnancies up to twenty-four weeks gestation.... Richard Nixon, Barry Goldwater, Gerald Ford, and George H.W. Bush were all pro-choice, and they were not party outliers. In 1972, a Gallup poll found that 68 percent of Republicans believed abortion to be a private matter between a woman and her doctor. The government, they said, should not be involved...&#x20;

Since the 1980s, opposition to abortion has become strongest in the party among [traditionalist Catholics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditionalist_Catholicism) and conservative Protestant evangelicals.[^cite_note-Williams-2022-194][^cite_note-Taylor-2018-408][^cite_note-Abdelfatah-2022-411] Initially, evangelicals were relatively indifferent to the cause of abortion and overwhelmingly viewed it as a concern that was [sectarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sectarianism) and [Catholic](606848.md).[^cite_note-Abdelfatah-2022-411] Historian [Randall Balmer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Randall_Balmer) notes that [Billy Graham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Billy_Graham)'s *[Christianity Today](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_Today)* published in 1968 a statement by theologian [Bruce Waltke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruce_Waltke) that:[^cite_note-412] "God does not regard the fetus as a soul, no matter how far gestation has progressed." Typical of the time, *Christianity Today* "refused to characterize abortion as sinful" and cited "individual health, family welfare, and social responsibility" as "justifications for ending a pregnancy."[^cite_note-Balmer-2022-413] Similar beliefs were held among conservative figures in the [Southern Baptist Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Baptist_Convention), including [W. A. Criswell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/W._A._Criswell), who is partially credited with starting the "[conservative resurgence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Baptist_Convention_conservative_resurgence)" within the organization, who stated: "I have always felt that it was only after a child was born and had a life separate from its mother that it became an individual person and it has always, therefore, seemed to me that what is best for the mother and for the future should be allowed." Balmer argues that evangelical American Christianity being inherently tied to opposition to abortion is a relatively new occurrence.[^cite_note-Balmer-2022-413][^cite_note-414] After the late 1970s, he writes, opinion against abortion among evangelicals rapidly shifted in favor of its prohibition.[^cite_note-Abdelfatah-2022-411]

Today, opinion polls show that Republican voters are heavily divided on the legality of abortion,[^cite_note-Doherty-2023-415] although the vast majority of the party's national and state candidates are [anti-abortion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-abortion) and oppose elective abortion on religious or moral grounds. While many advocate exceptions in the case of [incest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Incest), rape, or the mother's life being at risk, in 2012 the party approved a platform advocating banning abortions without exception.[^cite_note-platform-416] There were not highly polarized differences between the Democratic Party and the Republican Party before the *[Roe v. Wade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roe_v._Wade)* 1973 Supreme Court ruling (which made prohibitions on abortion rights unconstitutional), but after the Supreme Court ruling, opposition to abortion became an increasingly key national platform for the Republican Party.[^cite_note-The_Great_Divide:_Religious_and_Cultural_Conflict_in_American_Party_Politics2-417][^cite_note-How_race_and_religion_have_polarized_American_voters-418][^cite_note-419] As a result, Evangelicals gravitated towards the Republican Party.[^cite_note-The_Great_Divide:_Religious_and_Cultural_Conflict_in_American_Party_Politics2-417][^cite_note-How_race_and_religion_have_polarized_American_voters-418] Most Republicans oppose government funding for abortion providers, notably [Planned Parenthood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planned_Parenthood).[^cite_note-ontheissues-420] This includes support for the [Hyde Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyde_Amendment).

Until its dissolution in 2018, [Republican Majority for Choice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Majority_for_Choice), an abortion rights PAC, advocated for amending the GOP platform to include pro-abortion rights members.[^cite_note-421]

The Republican Party has pursued policies at the national and state levels to restrict [embryonic stem cell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embryonic_stem_cell) research beyond the original lines because it involves the destruction of human [embryos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embryo).[^cite_note-422][^cite_note-423]

After [the overturning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dobbs_v._Jackson_Women's_Health_Organization) of *Roe v. Wade* in 2022, a majority of Republican-controlled states [passed near-total bans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_law_in_the_United_States_by_state) on abortion, rendering it largely illegal throughout much of the United States.[^cite_note-424][^cite_note-Politico_Abortion-425]

#### Affirmative action

See also: [Affirmative action in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affirmative_action_in_the_United_States)

Republicans generally oppose [affirmative action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affirmative_action), often describing it as a "[quota system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_quota)" and believing that it is not [meritocratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meritocratic) and is counter-productive socially, with critics arguing that it promotes *reverse discrimination*. According to a 2023 ABC poll, a majority of Americans (52%) and 75% of Republicans supported the Supreme Court's decision in [Students for Fair Admissions v. Harvard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Students_for_Fair_Admissions_v._Harvard) prohibiting race as a factor in college admissions, compared to only 26% of Democrats.[^cite_note-426]

The 2012 Republican national platform stated, "We support efforts to help low-income individuals get a fair chance based on their potential and individual merit; but we reject preferences, quotas, and set-asides, as the best or sole methods through which fairness can be achieved, whether in government, education, or corporate boardrooms...Merit, ability, aptitude, and results should be the factors that determine advancement in our society."[^cite_note-427][^cite_note-affirmativeaction-428][^cite_note-Eilperin-429][^cite_note-430]

#### Gun policy

Both major parties in the United States support policies on gun ownership, in comparison to a large majority of the world, which give [broad legality to the possession of private firearms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_to_keep_and_bear_arms).[^cite_note-431] The modern Republican Party is now often seen as more favorable to laissez-faire gun laws.[^cite_note-Astor-2022-432][^cite_note-433] The [National Rifle Association of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Rifle_Association_of_America), a [special interest group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advocacy_group) in support of gun ownership, has consistently aligned itself with the Republican Party.[^cite_note-434] Following gun control measures under the [Clinton administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Bill_Clinton), such as the [Violent Crime Control and Law Enforcement Act of 1994](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Violent_Crime_Control_and_Law_Enforcement_Act_of_1994), the Republicans allied with the NRA during the [Republican Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Revolution) in [1994](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1994_United_States_elections).[^cite_note-435] Since then, the NRA has consistently backed Republican candidates and contributed financial support.[^cite_note-Astor-2022-432]

Scholars note that Republicans and conservatives have historically supported forms of [gun control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gun_control) when [leftist groups](18499.md), including [Black Power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_power) and [feminist movements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feminism_in_the_United_States), embrace "guns as tools for resistance and self‐defense against systemic oppression".[^cite_note-436]

#### Criminal justice

See also: [Illegal drug trade in the United States](37389158.md), [Legality of cannabis by U.S. jurisdiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legality_of_cannabis_by_U.S._jurisdiction), [Capital punishment in the United States](412425.md), and [Incarceration in the United States](1021698.md)

The Republican Party has generally promoted strict anti-crime policies, such as [mandatory minimum sentences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandatory_minimum_sentences) and the death penalty.[^cite_note-Lancaster-2024-437][^cite_note-438][^cite_note-439] In the 2010s, however, prominent Republicans demonstrated some interest in [criminal justice reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criminal_justice_reform_in_the_United_States) designed to combat [mass incarceration](1021698.md), with President Trump signing the [First Step Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Step_Act), which expanded good behavior credits for perpetrators of most nonviolent crimes and required the U.S. Attorney General to develop a system to assess the recidivism risk of all federal prisoners.[^cite_note-440] By 2024, however, the Republican Party and its leaders had largely left behind its prior support for reform of the justice system.[^cite_note-Lancaster-2024-437] Republican elected officials have historically supported the [war on drugs](1181646.md). They generally oppose [legalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legalization_of_drugs) or decriminalization of drugs such as [marijuana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cannabis_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-Tesler-2022-441][^cite_note-442][^cite_note-443]

Opposition to the legalization of marijuana has softened significantly over time among Republican voters and politicians.[^cite_note-Kneeland-2016-35][^cite_note-444][^cite_note-445] A 2021 [*Quinnipiac*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quinnipiac_University) poll found that 62% of Republicans supported the legalization of recreational marijuana use and that net support for the position was +30 points.[^cite_note-Tesler-2022-441] Some Republican-controlled states have legalized medical and recreational marijuana in recent years.[^cite_note-NCSL_MMJ-446] In September 2024, then-candidate Donald Trump endorsed the legalization of recreational marijuana.[^cite_note-447]

#### Immigration

See also: [Remigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Remigration), [Immigration to the United States](15051.md), and [Illegal immigration to the United States](5044573.md)

| Avatar of Homeland SecurityAvatar of [Homeland Security](58236.md) | **[Homeland Security](58236.md)** (@DHSgov) tweeted: |
| --- | --- |

> The stakes have never been higher, and the goal has never been more clear: \
> \
> &#x20;Remigration now.

November 28, 2025[^cite_note-TweetDHSgov-448]&#x20;

The Republican Party has taken widely varying views on immigration throughout its history, but has generally and traditionally taken an anti-immigration and [nativist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nativism_\(politics\)) stance compared to the opposition.[^cite_note-Smith-2021-106] In the period between 1850 and 1870, the Republican Party was more opposed to immigration than the Democrats. The GOP's opposition was, in part, caused by its reliance on the support of anti-Catholic and anti-immigrant parties such as the [Know-Nothings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Know_Nothing). In the decades following the Civil War, especially in the 1880s, the Republican Party lessened its stance on immigration, as it represented the manufacturers in the northeast (who wanted additional labor); although during this period, the Democratic Party still came to be seen as the party of both American and foreign labor, and many religious Republicans [used anti-Irish and pro-Christian sentiments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanism). Starting in the early 1930s, the parties focused on [Mexican emigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emigration_from_Mexico), as the Democrats proposed a softer stance on Mexican immigration during the [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression) and [New Deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Deal), rather than Republicans under [Herbert Hoover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herbert_Hoover).[^cite_note-449][^cite_note-450]

The modern GOP opposes the large majority of immigration from non-Western countries, including the [Middle East](19323.md), [Africa](5334607.md), [Asia](689.md), and [Latin America](18524.md).[^cite_note-Klaas-2025-239][^cite_note-451] In 2016, [Donald Trump](4848272.md) proposed to build [a wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico%E2%80%93United_States_border_wall) along the southern border of the United States. Trump [immigration policies during his administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_policy_of_the_first_Donald_Trump_administration) included [a travel ban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_Order_13769) from multiple Muslim-majority countries, a [Remain in Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Remain_in_Mexico) policy for asylum-seekers, [a controversial family separation policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trump_administration_family_separation_policy), and attempting to end [DACA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deferred_Action_for_Childhood_Arrivals).[^cite_note-Baker-2020-160][^cite_note-452] The party largely opposes immigration reform.[^cite_note-453] The party's 2024 platform is opposed to immigration, and calls for the mass deportation of all illegal immigrants in the United States,[^cite_note-2024_Platform-454] which during Trump's second presidency was extended to immigrants without permanent legal status.[^cite_note-455] The platform also calls for eliminating [family-sponsored immigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Form_I-130), replacing the [employment-based preference framework](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_Act_of_1990#Employment-based_immigration) with a [points system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Points-based_immigration_system), and cutting federal funding for sanctuary cities.[^cite_note-456]

Since the [2024 presidential election](59568546.md), the party's leading figures have shifted towards supporting remigration.[^cite_note-Kneeland-2016-35][^cite_note-Shamim_20260506-36][^cite_note-Yousef-2025-37][^cite_note-Tharoor_20251130-38] In September, Donald Trump called for "remigration" of illegal immigrants to their home countries and suspending [refugee resettlement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refugees_in_the_United_States), also pledging to "do large deportations in [Springfield, Ohio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Springfield,_Ohio)", referring to the town's community of legal [Haitian immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haitians_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-457][^cite_note-458] The usage mainstreamed the term in the country.[^cite_note-Kneeland-2016-35] In May 2025 the [Department of State](31975.md) released a "reorganisation chart" that included the creation of an "[Office of Remigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_of_Remigration)".[^cite_note-459][^cite_note-460][^cite_note-461] As President, Trump once again endorsed "remigration" in June 2025 as "reversing the invasion" of illegal immigrants into the United States.[^cite_note-462] In July 2025 Trump claimed that the [One Big Beautiful Bill Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_Big_Beautiful_Bill_Act) would allow for remigration to be implemented.[^cite_note-463] [White House Deputy Chief of Staff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_House_Deputy_Chief_of_Staff) [Stephen Miller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Miller) has been described as an advocate for remigration within the Trump administration.[^cite_note-464] In October 2025, the [U.S. Department of Homeland Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Department_of_Homeland_Security) gained attention after tweeting "remigrate" from its official X account.[^cite_note-465]

The Trump administration [has brought Afrikaner South Africans to the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_South_African_refugee_program) and other European-ethnic refugees through its [refugee program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Refugee_Admissions_Program).[^cite_note-guard-12may2025-466] In May 2025, [Ramaphosa made a state visit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Trump%E2%80%93Ramaphosa_Oval_Office_meeting) to meet with President Donald Trump. During the press conference, Trump confronted him with [claims of Afrikaner genocide in South Africa](2406568.md), which was denied by Ramaphosa.[^cite_note-467]

#### LGBT issues

Main articles: [2020s anti-LGBT movement in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020s_anti-LGBT_movement_in_the_United_States) and [Transgender genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transgender_genocide)

The Republican Party is broadly opposed to [LGBT rights](3510770.md), including [same-sex marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same-sex_marriage_in_the_United_States), [expression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020s_anti-LGBTQ_movement_in_the_United_States), and [transgender rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legal_status_of_transgender_people).[^cite_note-Wyatt-Nichol-2025-468] The large majority of its state and county/equivalent chapter platforms oppose same-sex marriage.[^cite_note-Wyatt-Nichol-2025-468]

From the early 2000s to the mid-2010s, Republicans opposed [same-sex marriage](92656.md), while being divided on the issue of [civil unions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_union) and [domestic partnerships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_partnership) for same-sex couples.[^cite_note-469] During the 2004 election, [George W. Bush](3414021.md) campaigned prominently on a constitutional amendment to prohibit same-sex marriage; many believe it helped Bush win re-election.[^cite_note-Lerer-2020-470][^cite_note-471] In both [2004](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/108th_United_States_Congress)[^cite_note-472] and [2006](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/109th_United_States_Congress),[^cite_note-473] President Bush, Senate Majority Leader [Bill Frist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Frist), and House Majority Leader [John Boehner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Boehner) promoted the [Federal Marriage Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Marriage_Amendment), a proposed failed constitutional amendment which would legally restrict the definition of marriage to [heterosexual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heterosexuality) couples.[^cite_note-474][^cite_note-475][^cite_note-476]

As more states legalized same-sex marriage in the 2010s, Republicans increasingly supported allowing each state to decide its own marriage policy.[^cite_note-A_Shifting_Landscape-477] As of 2014, most state GOP platforms expressed opposition to same-sex marriage.[^cite_note-478] The [2016 GOP platform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Republican_National_Convention) defined marriage as "natural marriage, the union of one man and one woman," and condemned the Supreme Court's [ruling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obergefell_v._Hodges) legalizing same-sex marriages.[^cite_note-479][^cite_note-480] The 2020 platform, which reused the 2016 platform, retained the statements against same-sex marriage.[^cite_note-481][^cite_note-482][^cite_note-483] Following his election as president in 2016, Donald Trump stated that he had no objection to same-sex marriage or to the Supreme Court decision in *Obergefell v. Hodges*, but had previously promised to consider appointing a Supreme Court justice to roll back the constitutional right.[^cite_note-Lerer-2020-470][^cite_note-484] The Trump administration banned transgender individuals from service in the United States military and rolled back other protections for transgender people which had been enacted during the previous Democratic presidency.[^cite_note-485]

The Republican Party platform previously opposed the [inclusion of gay people in the military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_orientation_and_the_United_States_military) and opposed adding sexual orientation to the list of protected classes since 1992.[^cite_note-486][^cite_note-487][^cite_note-488] The Republican Party opposed the inclusion of [sexual preference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_preference) in anti-discrimination statutes from 1992 to 2004.[^cite_note-1992_Republican_Party_platform-489] The 2008 and 2012 Republican Party platform supported anti-discrimination statutes based on sex, race, age, religion, creed, disability, or national origin, but both platforms were silent on [sexual orientation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_orientation) and [gender identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_identity).[^cite_note-2012_Republican_Party_platform-490][^cite_note-2008_Republican_Party_platform-491] The 2016 platform was opposed to sex discrimination statutes that included the phrase "sexual orientation".[^cite_note-492][^cite_note-493] In the early 2020s, numerous Republican-led states [proposed or passed laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020s_anti-LGBT_movement_in_the_United_States) that have been described as anti-trans by critics,[^cite_note-n141-494][^cite_note-495] as well as laws limiting or banning [public performances of drag shows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drag_panic), and teaching schoolchildren about LGBT topics.[^cite_note-n141-494] The 2024 party platform removes all mention of same-sex marriage saying instead "Republicans will promote a Culture that values the Sanctity of Marriage." However, the platform does explicitly declare opposition to topics relating to gender identity being taught in public schools, opposition to [transgender women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transgender_women) participating in women's sports, and opposition to federal funding for [gender-affirming surgery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender-affirming_surgery).[^cite_note-496]

#### Voting rights

See also: [Voter identification laws in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voter_identification_laws_in_the_United_States)

Virtually all restrictions on voting have in recent years been implemented by Republicans. Republicans, mainly at the state level, argue that the restrictions (such as the purging of [voter rolls](1516039.md), limiting voting locations, and limiting [early](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_voting) and [mail-in](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postal_voting) voting) are vital to prevent [voter fraud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voter_fraud), saying that voter fraud is an underestimated issue in elections. Polling has found majority support for early voting, automatic voter registration, and voter ID laws among the general population.[^cite_note-497][^cite_note-498][^cite_note-499]

In defending their restrictions to voting rights, Republicans have made false and exaggerated claims about the extent of voter fraud in the United States; all existing research indicates that it is extremely rare,[^cite_note-500][^cite_note-Hakim-2018-501][^cite_note-Linker-2018-502] and civil and voting rights organizations often accuse Republicans of enacting restrictions to influence elections in the party's favor. Many laws or regulations restricting voting enacted by Republicans have been successfully challenged in court, with court rulings striking down such regulations and accusing Republicans of establishing them with a partisan purpose.[^cite_note-Hakim-2018-501][^cite_note-Linker-2018-502]

After the Supreme Court decision in *[Shelby County v. Holder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shelby_County_v._Holder)* rolled back aspects of the [Voting Rights Act of 1965](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voting_Rights_Act_of_1965), Republicans introduced cuts to early voting, purges of voter rolls, and imposition of strict voter ID laws.[^cite_note-Hakim-2018-501] The 2016 Republican platform advocated proof of citizenship as a prerequisite for registering to vote and photo ID as a prerequisite when voting.[^cite_note-503]

After Donald Trump and his [Republican allies made false claims of fraud](65776878.md) during the 2020 presidential election, Republicans launched a nationwide effort to [impose tighter election laws at the state level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_efforts_to_restrict_voting_following_the_2020_presidential_election).[^cite_note-504][^cite_note-505][^cite_note-Gardner-2021-506] Such bills are centered around limiting mail-in voting, strengthening voter ID laws, shortening early voting, eliminating [automatic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voter_registration_in_the_United_States#Automatic) and [same-day voter registration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voter_registration_in_the_United_States#Election_Day_/_same-day), curbing the use of [ballot drop boxes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ballot_drop_box), and allowing for increased purging of voter rolls.[^cite_note-Bill-Tracker-507][^cite_note-NYT-practices-508] Republicans in at least eight states have also introduced bills that would give lawmakers greater power over election administration, after they were unsuccessful in their attempts to overturn election results in [swing states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swing_state) won by Biden.[^cite_note-Gardner-2021-506][^cite_note-509]

Supporters of the bills argue they would improve election security and reverse temporary changes enacted during the [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md); they point to false claims of significant election fraud, as well as the substantial public distrust of the integrity of the 2020 election those claims have fostered,[^cite_note-512] as justification.[^cite_note-513][^cite_note-514][^cite_note-515] Political analysts say that the efforts amount to [voter suppression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voter_suppression), are intended to advantage Republicans by reducing the number of people who vote, and would disproportionately affect [minority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Race_and_ethnicity_in_the_United_States) voters.[^cite_note-516]

## Republican presidents

Further information: [List of presidents of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_presidents_of_the_United_States)

## Further reading

Main article: [Bibliography of the history of the Republican Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bibliography_of_the_history_of_the_Republican_Party)

- *The Almanac of American Politics 2022* (2022) details on members of Congress, and the governors: their records and election results; also state and district politics; revised every two years since 1975. [Details](https://www.amazon.com/Almanac-American-Politics-Richard-Cohen/dp/195237409X/). [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220107175827/https://www.amazon.com/Almanac-American-Politics-Richard-Cohen/dp/195237409X) January 7, 2022, at the [Wayback Machine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayback_Machine); see *[The Almanac of American Politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Almanac_of_American_Politics)*.

- *American National Biography* (20 volumes, 1999) covers all politicians no longer alive; online at many academic libraries and at [Wikipedia Library](https://wikipedialibrary.wmflabs.org/partners/84/) [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20201030170202/https://wikipedialibrary.wmflabs.org/partners/84/) October 30, 2020, at the [Wayback Machine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayback_Machine).

- Aberbach, Joel D., ed. and Peele, Gillian, ed. *Crisis of Conservatism?: The Republican Party, the Conservative Movement, and American Politics after Bush* (Oxford UP, 2011). 403pp

- Aistrup, Joseph A. *The Southern Strategy Revisited: Republican Top-Down Advancement in the South* (1996).

- Bauman, Dan, and Brock Read. "A Brief History of GOP Attempts to Kill the Education Dept" *Chronicle of Higher Education* (June 21, 2018)

- Black, Earl, and Merle Black. *The Rise of Southern Republicans* (2002).

- Bowen, Michael, *The Roots of Modern Conservatism: Dewey, Taft, and the Battle for the Soul of the Republican Party.* (U of North Carolina Press, 2011). xii, 254pp.

- Brennan, Mary C. *Turning Right in the Sixties: The Conservative Capture of the GOP* (1995).

- Conger, Kimberly H. *The Christian Right in Republican State Politics* (2010) 202 pages; focuses on Arizona, Indiana, and Missouri.

- [Coppins, McKay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McKay_Coppins) (2023). *[Romney: A Reckoning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romney:_A_Reckoning)*. New York: Scribner. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [9781982196202](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/9781982196202). [OCLC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OCLC_\(identifier\)) [1394141101](https://search.worldcat.org/oclc/1394141101).

- Crane, Michael. *The Political Junkie Handbook: The Definitive Reference Books on Politics* (2004) covers all the major issues explaining the parties' positions.

- Critchlow, Donald T. *The Conservative Ascendancy: How the Republican Right Rose to Power in Modern America* (2nd ed. 2011).

- Ehrman, John, *The Eighties: America in the Age of Reagan* (2005).

- Fauntroy, Michael K. *Republicans and the Black vote* (2007).

- Fried, J (2008). *Democrats and Republicans – Rhetoric and Reality*. New York: Algora Publishing.

- Frank, Thomas. *What's the Matter with Kansas? How Conservatives Won the Heart of America* (2005).

- [Frum, David.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Frum) *What's Right: The New Conservative Majority and the Remaking of America* (1996).

- Gould, Lewis L. *The Republicans : A History of the Grand Old Party* (2nd ed, 2014); First edition 2003 was entitled: *Grand Old Party: A History of the Republicans* [online](https://books.google.com/books?id=LlppAgAAQBAJ\&printsec=frontcover\&dq=gould+republicans\&hl=en\&newbks=1\&newbks_redir=1\&sa=X\&ved=2ahUKEwjfwoCvn9GSAxU4IEQIHTxkHncQ6AF6BAgIEAM); the standard scholarly history

- Hemmer, Nicole. *Partisans: The Conservative Revolutionaries Who Remade American Politics in the 1990s* (2022)

- [Judis, John B.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Judis) and [Ruy Teixeira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruy_Teixeira). *The Emerging Democratic Majority* (2004), two Democrats project social trends.

- Kabaservice, Geoffrey. *Rule and Ruin: The Downfall of Moderation and the Destruction of the Republican Party, From Eisenhower to the Tea Party* (2012) scholarly history [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-0199768400](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-0199768400).

- Kleppner, Paul, et al. *The Evolution of American Electoral Systems* (1983), applies the party systems model.

- Kurian, George Thomas ed. *The Encyclopedia of the Republican Party* (4 vol., 2002).

- Lamis, Alexander P. ed. *Southern Politics in the 1990s* (1999).

- Levendusky, Matthew. *The Partisan Sort: How Liberals Became Democrats and Conservatives Became Republicans* (2009). Chicago Studies in American Politics.

- Mason, Robert. *The Republican Party and American Politics from Hoover to Reagan* (2011).

- Mason, Robert and Morgan, Iwan (eds.) *Seeking a New Majority: The Republican Party and American Politics, 1960–1980.* (2013) Nashville, TN. Vanderbilt University Press. 2013.

- Mayer, George H. *The Republican Party, 1854–1966.* 2d ed. (1967); an older standard scholarly history; [online](https://archive.org/details/republicanparty100maye)

- McPherson, James M. (1988). [*Battle Cry of Freedom: The Civil War Era*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_Cry_of_Freedom:_The_Civil_War_Era). Oxford; New York: Oxford University Press. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-0195038637](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-0195038637).

- Oakes, James. *The Crooked Path to Abolition: Abraham Lincoln and the Antislavery Constitution* (W\.W. Norton, 2021).

- [Perlstein, Rick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rick_Perlstein). *Before the Storm: Barry Goldwater and the Unmaking of the American Consensus* (2002), broad account of 1964.

- Perlstein, Rick. *[Nixonland: The Rise of a President and the Fracturing of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nixonland:_The_Rise_of_a_President_and_the_Fracturing_of_America)* (2009).

- Reinhard, David W. *The Republican Right since 1945* (1983).

- Ruffini, Patrick.&#x20;*&#x20;Party of the people: Inside the multiracial populist coalition remaking the GOP* (2023) [online](https://books.google.com/books?id=eR-UEAAAQBAJ\&printsec=frontcover\&dq=Party+of+the+people++Ruffini\&hl=en\&newbks=1\&newbks_redir=1\&sa=X\&ved=2ahUKEwjvo-CjsdGSAxV5J0QIHSoPJCwQ6AF6BAgOEAM)

- Rutland, Robert Allen. *The Republicans: From Lincoln to Bush* (1996), popular history [online](https://books.google.com/books?id=hZfltNKnQoMC\&printsec=frontcover\&dq=Rutland+Robert+Allen+Republican\&hl=en\&newbks=1\&newbks_redir=1\&sa=X\&ved=2ahUKEwiOpav7sdGSAxXELkQIHcvWJJQQ6AF6BAgHEAM)

- [Sabato, Larry J.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Larry_Sabato) *Divided States of America: The Slash and Burn Politics of the 2004 Presidential Election* (2005).

- Sabato, Larry J. and Bruce Larson. *The Party's Just Begun: Shaping Political Parties for America's Future* (2001), textbook.

- [Schlesinger, Arthur Meier Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_M._Schlesinger_Jr.) ed. *History of American Presidential Elections, 1789–2000* (various multivolume editions, latest is 2001). Essays on the most important election are reprinted in Schlesinger, *The Coming to Power: Critical presidential elections in American history* (1972). [online editions](https://archive.org/search?query=title%3A%28%20History%20of%20American%20Presidential%20Elections%29%20AND%20creator%3A%28Schlesinger%29)

- Shafer, Byron E. and Anthony J. Badger, eds. *Contesting Democracy: Substance and Structure in American Political History, 1775–2000* (2001), essays by specialists on each time period:&#x20;

  - includes: "To One or Another of These Parties Every Man Belongs": 1820–1865 by [Joel H. Silbey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joel_H._Silbey); "Change and Continuity in the Party Period: 1835–1885" by Michael F. Holt; "The Transformation of American Politics: 1865–1910" by Peter H. Argersinger; "Democracy, Republicanism, and Efficiency: 1885–1930" by Richard Jensen; "The Limits of Federal Power and Social Policy: 1910–1955" by Anthony J. Badger; "The Rise of Rights and Rights Consciousness: 1930–1980" by James T. Patterson; and "Economic Growth, Issue Evolution, and Divided Government: 1955–2000" by Byron E. Shafer.

- Shafer, Byron, and Richard Johnston. *The End of Southern Exceptionalism* (2006), uses statistical election data and polls to argue that GOP growth was primarily a response to economic change.

- Steely, Mel. *The Gentleman from Georgia: The Biography of Newt Gingrich* Mercer University Press, 2000. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [0865546711](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/0865546711).

- Sundquist, James L. *Dynamics of the Party System: Alignment and Realignment of Political Parties in the United States* (1983).

- Wooldridge, Adrian and John Micklethwait. *[The Right Nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Right_Nation): Conservative Power in America* (2004).
