[^cite_note-RegVoters-6]: &#x20;Only includes voters registered as party affiliates in the District of Columbia and the 32 states which allow [voter registration with a specific party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voter_registration_in_the_United_States#Party_affiliation). Voters can also register as "independent", which has risen over time. In the United States, voter registration determines eligibility to vote in internal party elections such as primaries, rather than dues-paying membership as in the rest of the world. Voter registration states include [Alaska](624.md), [Arizona](21883824.md), [Arkansas](1930.md), [California](5407.md), [Colorado](5399.md), [Connecticut](6466.md), [Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware), [District of Columbia](108956.md), [Florida](18933066.md), [Idaho](14607.md), [Iowa](26810748.md), [Kansas](16716.md), [Kentucky](16846.md), [Louisiana](18130.md), [Maine](19977.md), [Maryland](18858.md), [Massachusetts](1645518.md), [Missouri](19571.md), [Nebraska](21647.md), [Nevada](21216.md), [New Hampshire](21134.md), [New Jersey](21648.md), [New Mexico](21649.md), [New York](8210131.md), [North Carolina](21650.md), [Oklahoma](22489.md), [Oregon](26811621.md), [Pennsylvania](23332.md), [South Dakota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Dakota), [Utah](31716.md), [West Virginia](32905.md) and [Wyoming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wyoming). The remaining states lean heavily Republican, so the total percentage of the population that's Democrat or Republican is much closer than these values depict.<sup>[\[503\]](#cite_note-529)</sup>

[^cite_note-12]: &#x20;Includes [Trumpism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trumpism)<sup>[\[6\]](#cite_note-Ball_2024-7)</sup><sup>[\[7\]](#cite_note-v075-8)</sup><sup>[\[8\]](#cite_note-i277-9)</sup><sup>[\[9\]](#cite_note-different-10)</sup><sup>[\[10\]](#cite_note-Dominant-11)</sup>

[^cite_note-IdeologyNeoNationalism-13]: &#x20;The GOP is classified as populist-nationalist,<sup>[\[9\]](#cite_note-different-10)</sup><sup>[\[176\]](#cite_note-V-Dem_Institute-183)</sup> more specificly neo-nationalist.<sup>[\[507\]](#cite_note-533)</sup><sup>[\[508\]](#cite_note-534)</sup><sup>[\[141\]](#cite_note-Zhou_12/8/2022-148)</sup>

[^cite_note-IdeologyConservatism-14]: &#x20;Political scientists had generally seen the party as upholding [American conservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatism_in_the_United_States) before 2017; it is now considered a minority view in the party.<sup>[\[71\]](#cite_note-Rovira_Kaltwasser-78)</sup><sup>[\[6\]](#cite_note-Ball_2024-7)</sup><sup>[\[7\]](#cite_note-v075-8)</sup><sup>[\[42\]](#cite_note-campani-49)</sup><sup>[\[112\]](#cite_note-Biebricher-2023-119)</sup><sup>[\[113\]](#cite_note-Arhin-2023-120)</sup><sup>[\[114\]](#cite_note-Punchbowl_Old_GOP-121)</sup><sup>[\[117\]](#cite_note-washingtonpost.com-124)</sup><sup>[\[97\]](#cite_note-New_Fusionism-104)</sup><sup>[\[118\]](#cite_note-Cohn_12252024-125)</sup><sup>[\[115\]](#cite_note-Price_of_Power-122)</sup><sup>[\[123\]](#cite_note-Cliffe_2023-130)</sup><sup>[\[45\]](#cite_note-forgot-52)</sup><sup>[\[46\]](#cite_note-now-53)</sup><sup>[\[9\]](#cite_note-different-10)</sup><sup>[\[504\]](#cite_note-Reagan-530)</sup>

[^cite_note-IdeologyConservatismMinority-15]: &#x20;[Reagan-era conservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_positions_of_Ronald_Reagan), generally considered to be a leading faction of the party that is distinct from national-populism, is now a minority.<sup>[\[9\]](#cite_note-different-10)</sup><sup>[\[504\]](#cite_note-Reagan-530)</sup> Due to the hegemonic historical influence of [classical liberalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_liberalism) on American conservatism, scholars disagree on whether [Trumpism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trumpism) is a form of, outgrowth, or deviation away from the ideology, a subject discussed in [Max Boot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Max_Boot)'s *[Reagan: His Life and Legend](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reagan:_His_Life_and_Legend)*.<sup>[\[9\]](#cite_note-different-10)</sup><sup>[\[505\]](#cite_note-531)</sup> The GOP continues to generally adhere to [socially conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socially_conservative) positions, and scholars have noted analogies between Trumpism and [national conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_conservative) parties.<sup>[\[506\]](#cite_note-532)</sup><sup>[\[9\]](#cite_note-different-10)</sup><sup>[\[176\]](#cite_note-V-Dem_Institute-183)</sup>

[^cite_note-InternationalECRP-22]: &#x20;The Republican Party is a "global partner" of the [European Conservatives and Reformists Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Conservatives_and_Reformists_Party), a [European political party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_political_party).<sup>[\[509\]](#cite_note-535)</sup>

[^cite_note-USHouseCount-23]: &#x20;218 representatives are members of the party; however, one [independent](2172378.md) member, [Kevin Kiley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kevin_Kiley_\(politician\)), caucuses with the Republicans.

[^cite_note-Establishment-188]: &#x20;Attributed to multiple sources.<sup>[\[113\]](#cite_note-Arhin-2023-120)</sup><sup>[\[99\]](#cite_note-Smith-2021-106)</sup><sup>[\[112\]](#cite_note-Biebricher-2023-119)</sup><sup>[\[100\]](#cite_note-Ward_08-26-22-107)</sup><sup>[\[114\]](#cite_note-Punchbowl_Old_GOP-121)</sup><sup>[\[178\]](#cite_note-Kight_Feb142024-185)</sup><sup>[\[179\]](#cite_note-wsj.com-186)</sup><sup>[\[180\]](#cite_note-Gerstle2022-187)</sup>

[^cite_note-225]: &#x20;One example is [Steven Levitsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steven_Levitsky).<sup>[\[216\]](#cite_note-224)</sup>

[^cite_note-227]: &#x20;Political scientist [Cas Mudde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cas_Mudde) discusses this debate in his 2019 book *The Far-Right Today*.<sup>[\[217\]](#cite_note-226)</sup>

[^cite_note-populism-228]: &#x20;Scholars broadly agree that the modern GOP is a [neo-nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-nationalist) party.<sup>[\[176\]](#cite_note-V-Dem_Institute-183)</sup><sup>[\[c\]](#cite_note-IdeologyNeoNationalism-13)</sup> There is an ongoing academic debate on type, and they have variously described the party as [civic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civic_nationalist), [cultural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_nationalism), or [ethnic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_nationalist) nationalist,<sup>[\[i\]](#cite_note-225)</sup> or taking aspects from them.<sup>[\[j\]](#cite_note-227)</sup> In 2026, the [V-Dem Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V-Dem_Institute) reported a consensus in political science that the party is "far-right" and its nationalism is "anti-pluralist".<sup>[\[176\]](#cite_note-V-Dem_Institute-183)</sup>

[^cite_note-241]: &#x20;In 2026, the [V-Dem Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V-Dem_Institute) stated that there was a broad academic consensus that the GOP is located on the far-right of the international [political spectrum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_spectrum).<sup>[\[176\]](#cite_note-V-Dem_Institute-183)</sup> Political scientists and historians who hold to this stance, outside of comparative politics studies, include [Cas Mudde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cas_Mudde),<sup>[\[223\]](#cite_note-234)</sup> [Pippa Norris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pippa_Norris),<sup>[\[21\]](#cite_note-Colom-2024-28)</sup> [Lee Drutman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Drutman),<sup>[\[224\]](#cite_note-235)</sup> [Steven Levitsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steven_Levitsky),<sup>[\[225\]](#cite_note-236)</sup> [Roger Griffin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_Griffin),<sup>[\[226\]](#cite_note-237)</sup> [Robert Paxton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Paxton),<sup>[\[227\]](#cite_note-238)</sup> [Brian Klaas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brian_Klaas),<sup>[\[228\]](#cite_note-Klaas-2025-239)</sup> [Cristóbal Rovira Kaltwasser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crist%C3%B3bal_Rovira_Kaltwasser?action=edit\&redlink=1),<sup>[\[71\]](#cite_note-Rovira_Kaltwasser-78)</sup> [Kenneth Roberts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kenneth_Roberts_\(political_scientist\)),<sup>[\[71\]](#cite_note-Rovira_Kaltwasser-78)</sup> [Staffan I. Lindberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Staffan_I._Lindberg),<sup>[\[176\]](#cite_note-V-Dem_Institute-183)</sup> and [Jan-Werner Müller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jan-Werner_M%C3%BCller).<sup>[\[229\]](#cite_note-Jan-240)</sup>

[^cite_note-316]: &#x20;Right-to-work laws ban [union security agreements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_security_agreement), which require all workers in a unionized workplace to pay dues or a fair-share fee regardless of whether they are members of the union or not.<sup>[\[303\]](#cite_note-315)</sup>

[^cite_note-512]: &#x20;According to an NPR/PBS NewsHour/Marist poll, while more than 60% of Americans believe the 2020 election was secure, a large majority of Republican voters say they do not trust the results of the 2020 election.<sup>[\[497\]](#cite_note-NPR-poll-510)</sup> According to a poll by [Quinnipiac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quinnipiac_University), 77% of Republicans believe there was widespread voter fraud.<sup>[\[498\]](#cite_note-511)</sup>

[^cite_note-tie1-520]: &#x20;Republican Vice President [Dick Cheney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dick_Cheney) provided a [tie-breaking vote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tie-breaking_votes_cast_by_the_vice_president_of_the_United_States), initially giving Republicans a majority from [Inauguration Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_inauguration_of_George_W._Bush) until [Jim Jeffords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Jeffords) left the Republican Party to caucus with the Democrats on June 6, 2001.

[^cite_note-tie2-521]: &#x20;Democratic Vice President [Kamala Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris) provided a [tie-breaking vote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tie-breaking_votes_cast_by_the_vice_president_of_the_United_States), giving Democrats a majority from [Inauguration Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inauguration_of_Joe_Biden) until the end of the [117th Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/117th_Congress).

[^cite_note-525]: &#x20;Incumbent vice-president [James S. Sherman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_S._Sherman) was re-nominated as Taft's running-mate, but died six days before the election. Butler was chosen to receive the Republican vice-presidential votes after the election.

[^cite_note-522]: &#x20;Lincoln and Johnson were nominated by the [National Union Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Union_Party_\(United_States\)), a [wartime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War) coalition of Republicans, [War Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_Democrat), and [Unconditional Unionists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unconditional_Union_Party).

[^cite_note-523]: &#x20;Although Hayes won a majority of votes in the Electoral College, Democrat [Samuel J. Tilden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_J._Tilden) won a majority of the popular vote.

[^cite_note-524]: &#x20;Although Harrison won a majority of votes in the Electoral College, Democrat [Grover Cleveland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grover_Cleveland) won a plurality of the popular vote.

[^cite_note-526]: &#x20;Taft finished in third place in both the electoral and popular vote, behind [Progressive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressive_Party_\(United_States,_1912\)) [Theodore Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodore_Roosevelt).

[^cite_note-527]: &#x20;Although Bush won a majority of votes in the Electoral College, Democrat [Al Gore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Gore) won a plurality of the popular vote.

[^cite_note-528]: &#x20;Although Trump won a majority of votes in the Electoral College, Democrat [Hillary Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton) won a plurality of the popular vote.

Retrieved February 5, 2025. In Western democracies conventional conservatism is foundering. How did this once-dominant political force become so diminished? ... The picture today is drastically different. In every one of those countries the moderate conservative tendency represented by those leaders has been sidelined in one way or another. It has either been displaced by more hard-line elements within the same party, or by another party farther to the right; or it has started cooperating with the hard-line right; or has been partly or wholly marginalised within the political system. In one notable case (Orbán) it has self-radicalised. In several countries more than one of these things has occurred. ... The traditional centre right of the postwar decades could do so by "bundling" moderate social conservatism (moderate by the standards of its day, at least) with the pro-business economic conservatism favoured by higher earners. **But today those two elements are coming apart: richer folk are more likely to have gone to university and be socially liberal, while social conservatism is more associated with poorer groups.** That puts centre-right politics in zugzwang: forced to move, but with no good options. It can emphasize its social conservatism and lose pro-business graduates to the centre, or play it down, shore up its support among those voters and lose social conservatives to the radical right.

The election of Donald Trump as American president in 2016 encouraged further interest in ideas of national self-sufficiency. ... **Trump's worldview was much closer to a neomercantilist one than an autarkist one,** but some of his supporters on the far right are more clearly in the latter camp.14 For example, in a 2020 publication from the Claremont Institute, Curtis Yarvin called for the promotion of an "isolationist" policy of "neo-Sakoku". Like some other past autarkists, he argued that a world of autarkic states would be more peaceful because the reasons for conflict would diminish (Yarvin 2020). The Trump administration also indirectly encouraged new interest in greater national self-sufficiency in other countries because of its protectionism and its broader "weaponization" of America's international economic relations (Farrell and Newman 2019).

In 1973 many of the most vocal opponents of abortion were northern Democrats who believed in an expanded social-welfare state and who wanted to reduce abortion rates through prenatal insurance and federally funded day care. In 2022, most anti-abortion politicians are conservative Republicans who are skeptical of such measures. What happened was a seismic religious and political shift in opposition to abortion that has not occurred in any other Western country.
